($\\alpha{=}1.0$: $-27.8{\\pm}1.6\\%$). $\\alpha{=}0.25$: 3-seed mean${\\pm}$std (seeds 42, 43, 44); $\\alpha{=}1.0$: 4-seed mean${\\pm}$std; other levels: single seed (seed=42).}\n\\label{tab:drift_summary}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n$\\alpha$ & Entropy & ECE & Distinct-1 & Distinct-2 & Perplexity \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.00 & $-5.1$ & $+51.7$ & $-13.8$ & $-9.8$ & $-21.7$ \\\\\n0.25 & $-4.7{\\pm}0.5$ & $+81.8{\\pm}15.9$ & $-40.3{\\pm}0.3$ & $-56.4{\\pm}0.7$ & $-18.1{\\pm}0.8$ \\\\\n0.50 & $-18.6$ & $+842.8$ & $-37.3$ & $-51.5$ & $+21.1$ \\\\\n0.75 & $-27.7$ & $+1809.5$ & $-32.7$ & $-41.3$ & $+182.1$ \\\\\n1.00 & $-60.8{\\pm}1.2$ & $+3780{\\pm}30$ & $-27.8{\\pm}1.6$ & $-34.0{\\pm}3.3$ & $+3674{\\pm}45$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Method Sensitivity Analysis}\n\\label{sec:method_sensitivity}\n\nWe open with Claim~3---method sensitivity---because it conditions the interpretation of all subsequent results.\nIf different statistical methods yield contradictory conclusions on the same data, any single-method analysis is unreliable by construction.\n\n\\paragraph{Five-method ablation.}\nWe apply five lead-lag detection methods to the $\\alpha{=}1.0$ condition (4~seeds, 16 canary$\\to$downstream pairs).\nThe fraction of pairs reaching BH-corrected FDR significance varies dramatically by method: naive cross-correlation \\textbf{16/16} (100\\%), differenced cross-correlation \\textbf{14/16} (87.5\\%), threshold onset detection \\textbf{9/16} (56.3\\%), permutation Granger causality \\textbf{6/16} (37.5\\%), and Toda--Yamamoto Granger \\textbf{4/16} (25.0\\%).\nMethod choice alone swings significance from \\textbf{100\\% to 25\\%}: 50--57\\% of pairs classified as significant under one method flip to non-significant under another.\nThe spurious-signal rate---the fraction of pairs detected by the most liberal method but not confirmed by the most conservative---is \\textbf{75\\%} across all five methods and \\textbf{62.5\\%} when excluding Toda--Yamamoto, whose low statistical power at $T{=}10$ inflates this rate (\\S\\ref{sec:consensus_validation}).\nTable~\\ref{tab:method_comparison} reports significance rates restricted to 8 validated canary$\\to$downstream pairs (excluding failed candidates); the 16-pair rates above include effective rank and Self-BLEU to capture the full method-sensitivity landscape.\n\n\\paragraph{First-differencing diagnostic.}\nA single preprocessing decision can eliminate an entire analysis channel.\nFor canary$\\to$diversity pairs at $\\alpha{=}1.0$, all pairs retain unanimous FDR significance after first-differencing (24/24$\\to$24/24), confirming genuine temporal precedence rather than shared-trend confounding.\nFor canary$\\to$perplexity, however, undifferenced tests find near-universal significance ($F{=}16$--$36$) while differenced tests find \\textbf{zero} significant pairs ($F{\\approx}0$, $p{=}0.99$; Figure~\\ref{fig:differencing_dmax}a).\nThe apparent canary$\\to$perplexity relationship is entirely an artifact of co-trending.\n\n\\paragraph{Augmentation order sensitivity.}\nToda--Yamamoto Granger tests show extreme sensitivity to the augmentation order $d_{\\max}$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:differencing_dmax}b).\nAt $d_{\\max}{=}0$ (most permissive), approximately 6\\% of pairs are rejected; at adaptive $d_{\\max}$ determined by ADF unit-root tests, 75\\%; at $d_{\\max}{=}1$ (most conservative), \\textbf{94\\%}.\nThis single hyperparameter swings conclusions from near-universal significance to near-universal non-significance.\n\n\\paragraph{Inter-method agreement.}\nCohen's $\\kappa$, averaged over all method pairs, is \\textbf{0.185} (near chance; poor agreement); this value is consistent across contamination levels ($\\alpha{=}0.0$ through $\\alpha{=}1.0$), indicating that method disagreement is a structural property of the analysis rather than a regime-specific artifact.\nCross-architecture validation on GPT-2-medium (12 canary$\\to$downstream pairs) yields Fleiss' $\\kappa{=}0.091$, also in the ``slight agreement'' range, confirming that low inter-method agreement is not architecture-specific.\nRestricting to three informative methods (excluding naive cross-correlation and Toda--Yamamoto, which anchor the permissive and conservative extremes respectively) raises $\\kappa$ to 0.667 (substantial agreement), indicating that the low full-panel $\\kappa$ is driven by the deliberate inclusion of methods spanning the entire permissive-to-conservative spectrum rather than by fundamental incoherence among methods.\nTable~\\ref{tab:method_comparison} extends the analysis across contamination levels.\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.0$ (no synthetic data, serving as null control), naive cross-correlation finds \\textbf{100\\%} of pairs significant---a textbook spurious-regression outcome in trending time series.\nPermutation Granger and Toda--Yamamoto correctly find 0\\%.\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.50$, correlation-based methods (naive, differenced, threshold) unanimously find significance while causal methods unanimously find non-significance (Figure~\\ref{fig:consensus_heatmap}), the most extreme method$\\times$regime divergence in our experiments.\n\n\\paragraph{Method$\\times$regime interaction.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:method_comparison} reveals that the correlation-versus-causal divergence is not uniform across contamination levels.\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.25$, differenced cross-correlation drops to 25\\% and threshold onset to 37.5\\%, while naive cross-correlation remains at 100\\%---demonstrating that even partial detrending removes marginal associations at low contamination.\nIn low-contamination regimes ($\\alpha{\\leq}0.50$), the false confirmation rate for the most liberal method approaches 100\\%, while all Granger-type tests correctly find 0\\%---a regime where single-method analysis is maximally dangerous.\nThe graded response across methods, combined with the block structure visible in Figure~\\ref{fig:consensus_heatmap}, motivates the regime-dependent analysis in \\S\\ref{sec:regime}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{FDR significance rates (\\%) for five lead-lag methods across contamination levels (Pythia-410M). Method choice swings significance from 100\\% (naive cross-correlation) to 0\\% (permutation Granger) at $\\alpha{\\leq}0.50$, demonstrating that single-method conclusions are unreliable. Each cell reports the fraction of 8 canary$\\to$downstream pairs (token entropy and ECE $\\times$ 4 downstream metrics) reaching BH-corrected significance ($q{=}0.05$), excluding failed candidates (\\S\\ref{sec:case_study}). Seed coverage: $\\alpha{=}1.0$: 4 seeds (32 pairs); $\\alpha{=}0.25$ Toda--Yamamoto: 3 seeds (24 pairs), other methods single seed; remaining columns: single seed. $\\alpha{=}0.75$ analyzed separately (\\S\\ref{sec:regime}).}\n\\label{tab:method_comparison}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & $\\alpha{=}0.0$ & $\\alpha{=}0.25$ & $\\alpha{=}0.50$ & $\\alpha{=}0.75$ & $\\alpha{=}1.0$ & FPR$_\\text{null}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nNaive XCorr     & 100   & 100  & 100  & ---  & 100   & 100  \\\\\nDiff.\\ XCorr   & 37.5  & 25   & 100  & ---  & 100   & 37.5 \\\\\nThreshold       & 62.5  & 37.5 & 100  & ---  & 100   & 62.5 \\\\\nPerm.\\ Granger  & 0     & 0    & 0    & ---  & 75    & 0    \\\\\nToda--Yamamoto  & 0     & 12.5 & 0    & ---  & 59.4  & 0    \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Regime-Dependent Detectability}\n\\label{sec:regime}\n\nBeyond method choice, the contamination level~$\\alpha$ determines whether a temporal lead exists to detect.\nWe present regime effects at two levels: raw metric drift (method-independent) and Granger-precedence detectability.\n\n\\paragraph{Raw drift.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:drift_summary} shows that diversity collapse occurs at \\emph{all} contamination levels, not only under full synthetic replacement.\nDistinct-1 declines by 13.8\\% even at $\\alpha{=}0.0$ (attributable to fine-tuning on repeated real-data subsets), and more steeply at intermediate contamination: $-40.3{\\pm}0.3\\%$ ($\\alpha{=}0.25$), $-37.3\\%$ ($\\alpha{=}0.50$), $-32.7\\%$ ($\\alpha{=}0.75$), and $-27.8{\\pm}1.6\\%$ ($\\alpha{=}1.0$).\nThe non-monotonic pattern---diversity declining \\emph{more} at moderate~$\\alpha$ than at extreme~$\\alpha$---is confirmed by 3-seed replication at $\\alpha{=}0.25$ (std${=}0.3\\%$), indicating that this relationship between contamination level and collapse severity is robust rather than a seed-specific artifact.\n\n\\paragraph{Granger-precedence detectability.}\nDespite ubiquitous drift, causal detectability is sharply regime-dependent (Figure~\\ref{fig:regime_map}).\nPermutation Granger tests (first-differenced, BH-corrected) find 0/8 pairs FDR-significant at $\\alpha{=}0.00$, 0/8 at $\\alpha{=}0.25$, 0/8 at $\\alpha{=}0.50$, and \\textbf{6/8} at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ in the cross-$\\alpha$ pooled analysis.\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.75$ (single seed, seed=42), 3/8 pairs reach FDR significance; this result requires multi-seed replication before serving as primary evidence.\nWithin-$\\alpha$ multi-seed analysis at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ yields \\textbf{24/24} canary$\\to$diversity pairs FDR-significant across all four seeds, with 0/24 in the reverse direction, confirming temporal asymmetry.\nThe detectability boundary---the regime below which canary metrics no longer develop sufficient temporal lead for causal inference---lies at $\\alpha \\approx 0.50$--$0.75$, consistent with theoretical expectations about signal-to-noise limits in short time series \\cite{boettiger2012limits}.\nThis boundary is sharp rather than gradual: method-level consensus ($K{\\geq}4/5$) identifies 0--1 of 16 candidate pairs as robust at $\\alpha{\\leq}0.50$ but 11/16 at $\\alpha{=}0.75$ (single seed, pre-pipeline filtering; the stricter $K{\\geq}4/5$, $S{\\geq}3/4$ criterion at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ retains 6/16 after excluding failed candidates and spurious channels per \\S\\ref{sec:case_study}).\nThe lower count at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ despite stronger contamination reflects the stricter validation standard: multi-seed replication ($S{\\geq}3/4$) filters seed-specific artifacts, and pipeline filtering removes the perplexity channel (spurious per \\S\\ref{sec:method_sensitivity}) and failed canary candidates (effective rank, Self-BLEU), both of which contribute to the $\\alpha{=}0.75$ single-seed count.\nCross-architecture validation on GPT-2-medium confirms this boundary: at $\\alpha{=}0.25$, 0/8 pairs reach Confirmatory consensus ($K{\\geq}4/5$) despite substantially stronger canary drift (entropy $-32.8\\%$ vs.\\ Pythia $-4.7{\\pm}0.5\\%$), while at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ all 6 canary$\\to$diversity pairs reach $K{\\geq}4/5$ (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_arch_regime} summarizes the regime map across both architectures.\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.25$, GPT-2-medium yields 1/8 pairs at Tier~3 ($K{\\geq}4/5$) and 7/8 at Tier~2 ($K{\\geq}3/5$)---consistent with Pythia's 0/8 Tier~3, 0/8 Tier~2 result at the same contamination level, placing both architectures in the null/low-detectability regime (\\texttt{gen\\_wiki\\_gpt2m\\_a025\\_s42}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-architecture regime map: lead-lag consensus tier and metric drift at $\\alpha{=}0.25$ and $\\alpha{=}1.0$. Tier~3: $K{\\geq}4/5$ methods; Tier~2: $K{\\geq}3/5$. Drift is generation~0$\\to$10. GPT-2-medium $\\alpha{=}0.25$: single seed (seed=42, \\texttt{gen\\_wiki\\_gpt2m\\_a025\\_s42}); Pythia $\\alpha{=}0.25$: 3-seed mean${\\pm}$std. The regime boundary ($\\alpha{\\approx}0.50$--$0.75$) is consistent across two transformer decoder-only model families of similar scale.}\n\\label{tab:cross_arch_regime}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccccc}\n\\toprule\n$\\alpha$ & Model & Entropy & ECE & Distinct-1 & Distinct-2 & Perm.\\ Granger & Tier~3 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{0.25} & Pythia-410M & $-4.7{\\pm}0.5$ & $+81.8{\\pm}15.9$ & $-40.3{\\pm}0.3$ & $-56.4{\\pm}0.7$ & 0/8 & 0/8 \\\\\n                      & GPT-2-medium & $-32.8$ & $+422$ & $-23.5$ & $-31.6$ & 0/8 & 1/8 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{1.00} & Pythia-410M & $-60.8{\\pm}1.2$ & $+3780{\\pm}30$ & $-27.8{\\pm}1.6$ & $-34.0{\\pm}3.3$ & 6/8 & 6/6 \\\\\n                      & GPT-2-medium & \\multicolumn{5}{c}{(reported in \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}; all 6 canary$\\to$diversity Tier~3)} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Synchronous collapse at $\\alpha{=}0.50$.}\nThe $\\alpha{=}0.50$ regime provides particularly informative evidence.\nCorrelation-based methods unanimously find significance (Table~\\ref{tab:method_comparison}) while causal methods unanimously find non-significance.\nThis extreme method$\\times$regime interaction arises because canary and downstream metrics collapse \\emph{synchronously} at moderate contamination, preserving high correlation but eliminating the temporal lead required for Granger-precedence inference.\nWe highlight this as a \\emph{positive finding}: it delineates the regime boundary where naive analysis is most dangerous---high correlation coexists with zero causal precedence, maximizing the risk of spurious conclusions.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:consensus_heatmap} visualizes this interaction across all method$\\times\\alpha$ combinations, revealing a clear block structure that separates correlation-based methods from causal methods as a function of regime.\n\n\n\\subsection{Case Study: Canary Prediction of Diversity Collapse}\n\\label{sec:case_study}\n\nHaving established method sensitivity (Claim~3) and regime dependence (Claim~2), we present the conditions under which canary prediction succeeds (Claim~1).\nWe scope this claim precisely: canary metrics predict \\emph{diversity collapse} (declining distinct-$n$), not model collapse broadly.\n\n\\paragraph{Full synthetic replacement ($\\alpha{=}1.0$, 4 seeds).}\nToken entropy exhibits robust Granger-precedence over diversity collapse metrics, confirmed by 4 independent statistical methods (permutation Granger power${=}1.0$), 4 seeds, and cross-architecture replication on GPT-2-medium (Tier~3 consensus; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\nCross-correlation peaks at lag$={+}1$ with $|r|{>}0.99$ (cross-seed standard deviation: 0.0), establishing a consistent one-generation lead.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:metric_trajectory} illustrates the one-generation offset between token entropy decline and distinct-1 decline, visible across all four seeds.\nPermutation Granger tests (first-differenced) confirm all canary$\\to$diversity pairs as FDR-significant across all four seeds, including distinct-3 (Appendix~\\ref{app:distinct3}).\nReverse-direction tests (downstream$\\to$canary) find \\textbf{0/24} FDR-significant (all $p{>}0.49$), establishing complete directional asymmetry.\n\n\\paragraph{High contamination ($\\alpha{=}0.75$, single seed).}\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.75$ (seed=42), three of four forward-direction pairs reach FDR significance: ECE$\\to$distinct-1 ($p_\\text{fdr}{=}0.046$), ECE$\\to$distinct-2 ($p_\\text{fdr}{=}0.046$), and token entropy$\\to$distinct-2 ($p_\\text{fdr}{=}0.048$).\nToken entropy$\\to$distinct-1 is borderline ($p_\\text{fdr}{=}0.054$).\nAll four reverse directions are non-significant ($p{>}0.49$).\nThese single-seed results require multi-seed confirmation before serving as primary evidence.\n\n\\paragraph{ECE as a conditional secondary canary.}\nECE performs equivalently to token entropy across four of five methods (naive, differenced, threshold, permutation Granger).\nIts classification as ``secondary'' is driven entirely by Toda--Yamamoto Type~II errors at $T{=}10$, where bootstrap power analysis yields estimated power of 0.05--0.20 depending on $d_{\\max}$.\nResolving ECE's true canary status requires longer time series.\n\n\\paragraph{Failed canary candidates.}\nNot all metrics serve as canaries.\nEffective rank and Self-BLEU show no detectable lead-lag relationship with any downstream metric under any method or contamination level, and are excluded from the consensus framework.\nEffective rank behavior is model-dependent in an unexpected way: in Pythia-410M, effective rank decreases during collapse (representational contraction), whereas in GPT-2-medium at $\\alpha{=}0.25$ it \\emph{increases} from 2.37 to 8.48 (+258\\%; \\texttt{gen\\_wiki\\_gpt2m\\_a025\\_s42}).\nThe GPT-2-medium starting effective rank is near rank-2, suggesting the model's initial representation is already highly concentrated; iterative training disperses it rather than concentrating it further.\nThis opposite directionality across architectures precludes effective rank as a universal canary and motivates architecture-specific analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n\\paragraph{Consensus filtering.}\nOf 16 canary$\\to$downstream pairs tested at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (4 candidate canaries $\\times$ 4 downstream metrics), \\textbf{6 survive} the full consensus pipeline ($K{\\geq}4/5$ methods, $S{\\geq}3/4$ seeds): token entropy and ECE each predicting distinct-1 and distinct-2 (4 pairs; distinct-3 results in Appendix~\\ref{app:distinct3} bring the total to~6).\nThe 10 eliminated pairs comprise all canary$\\to$perplexity relationships (identified as spurious by the first-differencing diagnostic) and all effective-rank and Self-BLEU pairs (failed canaries).\nThe consensus framework reduces the naive 100\\% significance rate to the 37.5\\% rate supported by the most conservative individual methods (Figure~\\ref{fig:consensus_workflow}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Consensus Framework Validation}\n\\label{sec:consensus_validation}\n\nWe validate the consensus framework's calibration using built-in experimental controls.\n\n\\paragraph{False positive rate.}\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.0$, no synthetic data is injected; any detected lead-lag association is a false positive.\nIndividual-method FPR ranges from \\textbf{100\\%} (naive cross-correlation: 8/8 pairs) through 62.5\\% (threshold) and 37.5\\% (differenced) down to \\textbf{0\\%} (permutation Granger and Toda--Yamamoto).\nThe consensus framework at $K{\\geq}4/5$ achieves \\textbf{FPR${=}$0\\%}, matching the best individual methods while providing robustness against miscalibration of any single method (Table~\\ref{tab:fpr_fnr}).\n\n\\paragraph{False negative rate.}\nAt $\\alpha{=}1.0$, we define 6 effect-size reference pairs (token entropy and ECE $\\to$ distinct-1/2/3) using criteria independent of the consensus methods: drift ratio ${\\geq}3\\times$ the $\\alpha{=}0.0$ baseline (entropy $11.93\\times$, ECE $73.12\\times$) and canary onset strictly preceding downstream onset by ${\\geq}1$ generation (\\S\\ref{sec:calibration}).\nFNR is calibrated on a 4-method panel (excluding differenced cross-correlation to avoid circularity): at $K{\\geq}3/4$, $S{\\geq}3/4$, all~6 are recovered (\\textbf{FNR${=}$0\\%}).\nToda--Yamamoto alone yields FNR${=}$33.3\\% (2/6), missing ECE$\\to$distinct-2 and ECE$\\to$distinct-3 due to Type~II errors at $T{=}10$.\nWe note a design asymmetry: FPR is validated on method-only data (single seed at $\\alpha{=}0.0$), while FNR uses method$\\times$seed joint data (4~seeds at $\\alpha{=}1.0$).\nFull joint calibration---multi-seed null-control experiments---remains future work.\nTable~\\ref{tab:fpr_fnr} reports the calibration results.\n\n\\paragraph{Bootstrap power analysis.}\nTo quantify detection power beyond binary FNR, we perform bootstrap power analysis ($B{=}1{,}000$ resamples from 4-seed data at $\\alpha{=}1.0$).\nPermutation Granger achieves power${=}1.0$ on genuine pairs (canary$\\to$diversity) and power${=}0.0$ on spurious pairs (canary$\\to$perplexity), providing clean separation.\nToda--Yamamoto achieves power${=}0.05$--$0.20$ on genuine pairs (depending on $d_{\\max}$), confirming its poor sensitivity at $T{=}10$.\nWe note that bootstrap power${=}1.0$ reflects the effect size in our 4-seed data and should not be interpreted as a universal power guarantee; furthermore, the two methods use different data-generating processes for calibration (permutation Granger resamples observed $\\alpha{=}1.0$ trajectories, while Toda--Yamamoto power was estimated under a generic VAR(2) simulation with $\\beta{=}0.3$).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{False positive and false negative rates for individual methods and the consensus framework (Pythia-410M). The consensus threshold $K{\\geq}4/5$ achieves FPR${=}$0\\% and FNR${=}$0\\%, matching the best individual method (permutation Granger) while providing robustness against miscalibration. FPR: $\\alpha{=}0.0$ null control (single seed, $M{=}5$ methods). FNR: $\\alpha{=}1.0$ against 6 effect-size reference pairs (4-method panel excluding differenced cross-correlation).     "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Granger precedence is not causation.}\nGranger causality tests whether past values of one series improve prediction of another beyond its own history; it does not establish true causation~\\cite{granger1969investigating,shojaie2022granger}.\nBivariate Granger tests cannot exclude confounders: an unobserved process---such as a latent distributional shift in the training data---could independently drive both token entropy and diversity metrics, producing spurious lead-lag associations even when no direct relationship exists.\nOur multi-method consensus framework inherits this limitation in full.\nThroughout this paper, ``Granger precedence'' denotes predictive improvement in a time-series forecasting sense, not a causal mechanism.\nThis distinction is why we scope our central claim to ``token entropy Granger-precedes diversity collapse'' rather than asserting that token entropy \\emph{causes} diversity collapse.\n\n\\paragraph{Calibration scope and generalization.}\nAll FPR and FNR calibration results reported in this work are specific to our experimental setup: Pythia-410M~\\cite{biderman2023pythia}, a WikiText-103 subset, $T{=}10$ generation cycles, and five contamination levels ($\\alpha \\in \\{0.0, 0.25, 0.50, 0.75, 1.0\\}$).\nGeneralization to other model architectures, datasets, or training protocols requires independent validation.\nThe consensus framework's structure---multi-method agreement combined with multi-seed replication---is intended to be applicable beyond this specific setting, but the operating thresholds ($K{\\geq}4/5$ methods, $S{\\geq}3/4$ seeds) are empirically calibrated and may require re-tuning under different conditions.\nFor instance, architectures with different tokenizer granularity or training dynamics could shift the balance between false positive and false negative rates at these thresholds.\nPreliminary cross-architecture validation on GPT-2-medium (single seed, $\\alpha{=}1.0$) replicates the entropy-leads-diversity pattern at Tier~3 consensus: all 6 canary$\\to$diversity pairs reach $K{\\geq}4/5$ method agreement (permutation Granger $p_{\\text{FDR}}{=}0.003$), with 0/6 reverse-direction pairs significant.\nFull-panel inter-method agreement remains low (Fleiss' $\\kappa{=}0.091$) but rises to $\\kappa{=}0.667$ among the three informative methods (\\S\\ref{sec:method_sensitivity}), confirming that the consensus framework's structure transfers across architectures even if operating thresholds require architecture-specific calibration.\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.25$, GPT-2-medium yields 1/8 pairs at Tier~3 consensus ($K{\\geq}4/5$) and 7/8 at Tier~2 ($K{\\geq}3/5$), placing it in the same null/low-detectability regime as Pythia (0/8 Tier~3) and confirming that the detectability boundary is consistent across two transformer decoder-only model families of similar scale (\\texttt{gen\\_wiki\\_gpt2m\\_a025\\_s42}).\nThe full GPT-2-medium drift profile at $\\alpha{=}0.25$ is: entropy $-32.8\\%$, ECE $+422\\%$, perplexity $-44.5\\%$, distinct-1 $-23.5\\%$, distinct-2 $-31.6\\%$, distinct-3 $-30.2\\%$, MAUVE $0.0315 \\to 0.0282$ ($-10.5\\%$).\nCompared to Pythia ($-4.7{\\pm}0.5\\%$ entropy, $-40.3{\\pm}0.3\\%$ distinct-1), GPT-2-medium shows substantially stronger canary drift but milder diversity decline---likely due to its $8\\times$ larger batch size exposing more data per step.\nThe stronger canary signal approaches detectability (permutation Granger $F{=}64.09$, $p{=}0.081$ for entropy$\\to$distinct-1, compared to $p{>}0.5$ in Pythia), suggesting that the regime boundary may shift with increased effective sample size, but single-seed $T{=}10$ power remains insufficient for Confirmatory claims.\nToda--Yamamoto Granger finds 1/8 pairs significant, consistent with its known instability at short series lengths (\\S\\ref{sec:consensus_validation}).\n\n\\paragraph{FPR/FNR calibration asymmetry.}\nThe FPR and FNR estimates in our calibration analysis differ in their coverage of the seed dimension.\nFPR is validated on the method dimension only, using a single seed at the null-control condition ($\\alpha{=}0.0$).\nFNR is validated on the joint method${\\times}$seed dimension, aggregating across four seeds at full contamination ($\\alpha{=}1.0$).\nThis asymmetry means our FPR estimate of 0\\% may be optimistic: if seed-level variance in false positive rates is non-negligible, the true FPR under the consensus rule could exceed zero.\nFull joint calibration---requiring multi-seed null-control experiments at $\\alpha{=}0.0$---remains an open direction for future work.\n\n\\paragraph{Short time-series constraint.}\nWith $T{=}10$ generation cycles (11 data points per series), our time-series analyses operate near the lower bound of what standard methods require.\nThis series length is typical for iterative training studies, where each generation involves a complete training run, but it severely limits statistical power.\nThe Toda--Yamamoto procedure's elevated FNR (33.3\\%, missing 2 of 6 directional pairs) is attributable to insufficient residual degrees of freedom at this series length, not to a fundamental deficiency in the method itself.\nThis finding aligns with \\citet{boettiger2012limits}, who demonstrated that early-warning signals have limited detection power in short time series.\nLonger generation chains would improve power for all methods but impose substantial computational cost; this trade-off between statistical power and compute budget is inherent to iterative training research and cannot be resolved by methodological improvements alone.\nHowever, observation window sensitivity analysis provides reassurance: the $4/5$ consensus classification stabilizes at $T{\\geq}5$, well below our observation window of $T{=}10$, with identical results from $T{=}5$ through $T{=}10$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:window_sensitivity}).\nThe Toda--Yamamoto procedure produces valid test statistics at all window lengths ($T \\in \\{5, \\ldots, 10\\}$), but with unstable sensitivity---3/6 to 6/6 pairs reach significance depending on window length---confirming that its elevated FNR reflects limited residual degrees of freedom rather than absence of signal.\n\n\\paragraph{Effective rank direction is model-dependent.}\nEffective rank evolves in opposite directions across architectures during iterative training.\nIn Pythia-410M, effective rank decreases (representational contraction), consistent with mode collapse concentrating the output distribution into fewer dimensions.\nIn GPT-2-medium at $\\alpha{=}0.25$, effective rank \\emph{increases} from 2.37 to 8.48 (+258\\%; \\texttt{gen\\_wiki\\_gpt2m\\_a025\\_s42}).\nThe likely explanation is starting-point dependence: GPT-2-medium begins near rank-2 (a highly concentrated initial representation), and iterative training disperses activation mass across more dimensions rather than concentrating it further.\nThis finding demonstrates that effective rank cannot serve as a direction-agnostic canary and that its trajectory must be interpreted relative to architecture-specific baselines.\nWe note this as a caveat on the generality of representational metrics as collapse indicators, even when the regime map (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_arch_regime}) and lead-lag detectability are consistent across architectures.\n\n\\paragraph{Diversity--calibration dissociation.}\nDiversity metrics and calibration metrics respond to synthetic contamination in qualitatively opposite directions across the $\\alpha$ spectrum.\nDiversity collapse (distinct-$n$ decline) is most severe at low contamination ($\\alpha{=}0.25$: distinct-3 declines by $58.9{\\pm}0.9\\%$) and attenuates at higher levels ($\\alpha{=}1.0$: $-30.5\\%$), while calibration degradation (ECE) increases monotonically with $\\alpha$ ($+51.7\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}0.0$ to $+3780\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}1.0$).\nThis dissociation underscores the need for multi-method consensus: the regime in which a metric degrades most severely ($\\alpha{=}0.25$ for diversity) does not coincide with the regime in which its temporal lead is most detectable ($\\alpha{\\geq}0.75$), and a single metric family cannot capture the full picture of iterative training degradation.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical utility paradox.}\nThe canary framework exhibits a paradox: it works best where it is least needed.\nAt $\\alpha{\\geq}0.75$, token entropy reliably Granger-precedes diversity collapse---but at these contamination levels, degradation is already severe and likely detectable through simpler means.\nAt $\\alpha{\\leq}0.50$, where early warning would be most valuable, the canary signal is undetectable by any method tested.\nThis is not a framework deficiency but an inherent characteristic of collapse dynamics: at low contamination, diversity changes are too small relative to inter-generation noise for any temporal lead to emerge above the statistical detection floor~\\cite{boettiger2012limits}.\nThe practical implication is that the framework is better suited to continuous monitoring dashboards---tracking cumulative trend shifts across many generations---than to single-point threshold alarms.\nIf contamination grows over time (as in realistic web-scale data pipelines), the canary signal will strengthen as $\\alpha$ increases, providing earlier detection than post-hoc quality audits.\n\n\\paragraph{Token entropy versus perplexity.}\nThe finding that token entropy Granger-precedes diversity while perplexity does not ($F{\\approx}0$, $p{=}0.99$ after differencing) requires clarification, since perplexity is the exponential of cross-entropy and appears mathematically related to token entropy.\nThe distinction lies in the evaluation domain: our ``token entropy'' is $H(p_\\theta | \\text{context})$ evaluated on \\emph{held-out real data}---measuring the model's predictive uncertainty on genuine text---while ``perplexity'' is $\\exp(H_\\text{cross})$ evaluated on \\emph{model-generated text}---measuring the model's self-consistency on its own outputs.\nThe identity $\\text{ppl} = \\exp(\\text{entropy})$ holds only when the model distribution equals the data distribution, a condition that iterative collapse systematically violates.\nIn practice, both metrics trend downward during collapse (the model becomes more certain), producing high undifferenced correlation.\nFirst-differencing removes this shared trend: the canary$\\to$diversity channel survives because entropy \\emph{leads} diversity decline by one generation (the model narrows its distribution before output diversity measurably falls), whereas the canary$\\to$perplexity channel vanishes because entropy and perplexity co-move \\emph{synchronously}---both driven by the same underlying distributional contraction.\nThus, the perplexity channel being ``spurious'' is not trivially expected from the mathematical relationship, but rather a substantive finding about collapse dynamics: the generation-evaluation divergence breaks the entropy--perplexity coupling in a temporally informative way.\n\n\\paragraph{NLP-specific constraints on lead-lag analysis.}\nOur application of Granger-type methods to iterative training faces constraints absent in their original econometric settings.\nFirst, observation length: typical econometric applications use $T{=}100$--$1000$ time points, whereas iterative training studies are limited to $T{=}5$--$20$ generations due to computational cost.\nThis severely constrains degrees of freedom for VAR-based methods and explains the Toda--Yamamoto procedure's poor power in our setting.\nSecond, non-stationarity is by design: each generation's training data differs from the previous generation's, making the underlying process non-stationary by construction rather than as an incidental property.\nStandard stationarity tests (ADF, KPSS) may yield misleading results when applied to processes with deterministic degradation trajectories rather than stochastic unit roots.\nThird, the sample-size bottleneck compounds with multiple testing: with only 8--32 pairs per regime, FDR correction operates on small multiplicity, limiting its ability to separate true from false discoveries.\nThese constraints motivate our multi-method consensus approach as a complement to---rather than replacement for---traditional significance testing.\n\n\\paragraph{Future work.}\nSeveral directions extend this work.\nMultivariate VAR models would enable testing for confounders and multi-directional Granger relationships beyond our current bivariate framework.\nCross-architecture validation beyond our preliminary single-seed GPT-2-medium result requires multi-seed replication; evaluation on larger-scale models would further test generalizability.\nOnline monitoring---integrating canary metrics into training pipelines for real-time collapse detection---is a natural applied extension.\nAccumulate-mode experiments~\\cite{dey2024breaking} would test whether canary dynamics differ from the replace-mode setting studied here.\nFinally, connecting the empirically observed detectability boundary at $\\alpha{\\approx}0.50$--$0.75$ to theoretical phase transition analysis~\\cite{truong2025entropy} could yield a principled explanation for why canary signals emerge at this contamination regime rather than at lower contamination levels.",

```text
125	Within-$\alpha$ multi-seed analysis at $\alpha{=}1.0$ yields \textbf{24/24} canary$\to$diversity pairs FDR-significant across all four seeds, with 0/24 in the reverse direction, confirming temporal asymmetry.
126	The detectability boundary---the regime below which canary metrics no longer develop sufficient temporal lead for causal inference---lies at $\alpha \approx 0.50$--$0.75$, consistent with theoretical expectations about signal-to-noise limits in short time series \cite{boettiger2012limits}.
127	This boundary is sharp rather than gradual: method-level consensus ($K{\geq}4/5$) identifies 0--1 of 16 candidate pairs as robust at $\alpha{\leq}0.50$ but 11/16 at $\alpha{=}0.75$ (single seed, pre-pipeline filtering; the stricter $K{\geq}4/5$, $S{\geq}3/4$ criterion at $\alpha{=}1.0$ retains 6/16 after excluding failed candidates and spurious channels per \S\ref{sec:case_study}).
128	The lower count at $\alpha{=}1.0$ despite stronger contamination reflects the stricter validation standard: multi-seed replication ($S{\geq}3/4$) filters seed-specific artifacts, and pipeline filtering removes the perplexity channel (spurious per \S\ref{sec:method_sensitivity}) and failed canary candidates (effective rank, Self-BLEU), both of which contribute to the $\alpha{=}0.75$ single-seed count.
129	Cross-architecture validation on GPT-2-medium confirms this boundary: at $\alpha{=}0.25$, 0/8 pairs reach Confirmatory consensus ($K{\geq}4/5$) despite substantially stronger canary drift (entropy $-32.8\%$ vs.\ Pythia $-4.7{\pm}0.5\%$), while at $\alpha{=}1.0$ all 6 canary$\to$diversity pairs reach $K{\geq}4/5$ (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
130	Table~\ref{tab:cross_arch_regime} summarizes the regime map across both architectures.
131	At $\alpha{=}0.25$, GPT-2-medium yields 1/8 pairs at Tier~3 ($K{\geq}4/5$) and 7/8 at Tier~2 ($K{\geq}3/5$)---consistent with Pythia's 0/8 Tier~3, 0/8 Tier~2 result at the same contamination level, placing both architectures in the null/low-detectability regime (\texttt{gen\_wiki\_gpt2m\_a025\_s42}).
132	
133	\begin{table}[t]
134	\centering
135	\small
136	\caption{Cross-architecture regime map: lead-lag consensus tier and metric drift at $\alpha{=}0.25$ and $\alpha{=}1.0$. Tier~3: $K{\geq}4/5$ methods; Tier~2: $K{\geq}3/5$. Drift is generation~0$\to$10. GPT-2-medium $\alpha{=}0.25$: single seed (seed=42, \texttt{gen\_wiki\_gpt2m\_a025\_s42}); Pythia $\alpha{=}0.25$: 3-seed mean${\pm}$std. The regime boundary ($\alpha{\approx}0.50$--$0.75$) is consistent across two transformer decoder-only model families of similar scale.}
137	\label{tab:cross_arch_regime}
138	\begin{tabular}{llcccccc}
139	\toprule
140	$\alpha$ & Model & Entropy & ECE & Distinct-1 & Distinct-2 & Perm.\ Granger & Tier~3 \\
141	\midrule
142	\multirow{2}{*}{0.25} & Pythia-410M & $-4.7{\pm}0.5$ & $+81.8{\pm}15.9$ & $-40.3{\pm}0.3$ & $-56.4{\pm}0.7$ & 0/8 & 0/8 \\
143	                      & GPT-2-medium & $-32.8$ & $+422$ & $-23.5$ & $-31.6$ & 0/8 & 1/8 \\
144	\midrule
145	\multirow{2}{*}{1.00} & Pythia-410M & $-60.8{\pm}1.2$ & $+3780{\pm}30$ & $-27.8{\pm}1.6$ & $-34.0{\pm}3.3$ & 6/8 & 6/6 \\
146	                      & GPT-2-medium & \multicolumn{5}{c}{(reported in \S\ref{sec:discussion}; all 6 canary$\to$diversity Tier~3)} \\
147	\bottomrule
148	\end{tabular}
149	\end{table}
150	
151	\paragraph{Synchronous collapse at $\alpha{=}0.50$.}
152	The $\alpha{=}0.50$ regime provides particularly informative evidence.
153	Correlation-based methods unanimously find significance (Table~\ref{tab:method_comparison}) while causal methods unanimously find non-significance.
154	This extreme method$\times$regime interaction arises because canary and downstream metrics collapse \emph{synchronously} at moderate contamination, preserving high correlation but eliminating the temporal lead required for Granger-precedence inference.
155	We highlight this as a \emph{positive finding}: it delineates the regime boundary where naive analysis is most dangerous---high correlation coexists with zero causal precedence, maximizing the risk of spurious conclusions.
156	Figure~\ref{fig:consensus_heatmap} visualizes this interaction across all method$\times\alpha$ combinations, revealing a clear block structure that separates correlation-based methods from causal methods as a function of regime.
157	
158	
159	\subsection{Case Study: Canary Prediction of Diversity Collapse}
160	\label{sec:case_study}
161	
162	Having established method sensitivity (Claim~3) and regime dependence (Claim~2), we present the conditions under which canary prediction succeeds (Claim~1).
163	We scope this claim precisely: canary metrics predict \emph{diversity collapse} (declining distinct-$n$), not model collapse broadly.
164	
165	\paragraph{Full synthetic replacement ($\alpha{=}1.0$, 4 seeds).}
166	Token entropy exhibits robust Granger-precedence over diversity collapse metrics, confirmed by 4 independent statistical methods (permutation Granger power${=}1.0$), 4 seeds, and cross-architecture replication on GPT-2-medium (Tier~3 consensus; \S\ref{sec:discussion}).
167	Cross-correlation peaks at lag$={+}1$ with $|r|{>}0.99$ (cross-seed standard deviation: 0.0), establishing a consistent one-generation lead.
168	Figure~\ref{fig:metric_trajectory} illustrates the one-generation offset between token entropy decline and distinct-1 decline, visible across all four seeds.
169	Permutation Granger tests (first-differenced) confirm all canary$\to$diversity pairs as FDR-significant across all four seeds, including distinct-3 (Appendix~\ref{app:distinct3}).
```

```text
1	% 2026-05-16: Expanded GPT-2m α=0.25 cross-arch validation with full drift profile,
2	% tier breakdown (1/8 T3, 7/8 T2), TY 1/8 sig. Added "Effective rank direction is
3	% model-dependent" paragraph. Source: gen_wiki_gpt2m_a025_s42.
4	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
5	
6	\paragraph{Granger precedence is not causation.}
7	Granger causality tests whether past values of one series improve prediction of another beyond its own history; it does not establish true causation~\cite{granger1969investigating,shojaie2022granger}.
8	Bivariate Granger tests cannot exclude confounders: an unobserved process---such as a latent distributional shift in the training data---could independently drive both token entropy and diversity metrics, producing spurious lead-lag associations even when no direct relationship exists.
9	Our multi-method consensus framework inherits this limitation in full.
10	Throughout this paper, ``Granger precedence'' denotes predictive improvement in a time-series forecasting sense, not a causal mechanism.
11	This distinction is why we scope our central claim to ``token entropy Granger-precedes diversity collapse'' rather than asserting that token entropy \emph{causes} diversity collapse.
12	
13	\paragraph{Calibration scope and generalization.}
14	All FPR and FNR calibration results reported in this work are specific to our experimental setup: Pythia-410M~\cite{biderman2023pythia}, a WikiText-103 subset, $T{=}10$ generation cycles, and five contamination levels ($\alpha \in \{0.0, 0.25, 0.50, 0.75, 1.0\}$).
15	Generalization to other model architectures, datasets, or training protocols requires independent validation.
16	The consensus framework's structure---multi-method agreement combined with multi-seed replication---is intended to be applicable beyond this specific setting, but the operating thresholds ($K{\geq}4/5$ methods, $S{\geq}3/4$ seeds) are empirically calibrated and may require re-tuning under different conditions.
17	For instance, architectures with different tokenizer granularity or training dynamics could shift the balance between false positive and false negative rates at these thresholds.
18	Preliminary cross-architecture validation on GPT-2-medium (single seed, $\alpha{=}1.0$) replicates the entropy-leads-diversity pattern at Tier~3 consensus: all 6 canary$\to$diversity pairs reach $K{\geq}4/5$ method agreement (permutation Granger $p_{\text{FDR}}{=}0.003$), with 0/6 reverse-direction pairs significant.
19	Full-panel inter-method agreement remains low (Fleiss' $\kappa{=}0.091$) but rises to $\kappa{=}0.667$ among the three informative methods (\S\ref{sec:method_sensitivity}), confirming that the consensus framework's structure transfers across architectures even if operating thresholds require architecture-specific calibration.
20	At $\alpha{=}0.25$, GPT-2-medium yields 1/8 pairs at Tier~3 consensus ($K{\geq}4/5$) and 7/8 at Tier~2 ($K{\geq}3/5$), placing it in the same null/low-detectability regime as Pythia (0/8 Tier~3) and confirming that the detectability boundary is consistent across two transformer decoder-only model families of similar scale (\texttt{gen\_wiki\_gpt2m\_a025\_s42}).
21	The full GPT-2-medium drift profile at $\alpha{=}0.25$ is: entropy $-32.8\%$, ECE $+422\%$, perplexity $-44.5\%$, distinct-1 $-23.5\%$, distinct-2 $-31.6\%$, distinct-3 $-30.2\%$, MAUVE $0.0315 \to 0.0282$ ($-10.5\%$).
22	Compared to Pythia ($-4.7{\pm}0.5\%$ entropy, $-40.3{\pm}0.3\%$ distinct-1), GPT-2-medium shows substantially stronger canary drift but milder diversity decline---likely due to its $8\times$ larger batch size exposing more data per step.
23	The stronger canary signal approaches detectability (permutation Granger $F{=}64.09$, $p{=}0.081$ for entropy$\to$distinct-1, compared to $p{>}0.5$ in Pythia), suggesting that the regime boundary may shift with increased effective sample size, but single-seed $T{=}10$ power remains insufficient for Confirmatory claims.
24	Toda--Yamamoto Granger finds 1/8 pairs significant, consistent with its known instability at short series lengths (\S\ref{sec:consensus_validation}).
25	
26	\paragraph{FPR/FNR calibration asymmetry.}
27	The FPR and FNR estimates in our calibration analysis differ in their coverage of the seed dimension.
28	FPR is validated on the method dimension only, using a single seed at the null-control condition ($\alpha{=}0.0$).
29	FNR is validated on the joint method${\times}$seed dimension, aggregating across four seeds at full contamination ($\alpha{=}1.0$).
30	This asymmetry means our FPR estimate of 0\% may be optimistic: if seed-level variance in false positive rates is non-negligible, the true FPR under the consensus rule could exceed zero.
31	Full joint calibration---requiring multi-seed null-control experiments at $\alpha{=}0.0$---remains an open direction for future work.
32	
33	\paragraph{Short time-series constraint.}
34	With $T{=}10$ generation cycles (11 data points per series), our time-series analyses operate near the lower bound of what standard methods require.
35	This series length is typical for iterative training studies, where each generation involves a complete training run, but it severely limits statistical power.
```

```text
40	The Toda--Yamamoto procedure produces valid test statistics at all window lengths ($T \in \{5, \ldots, 10\}$), but with unstable sensitivity---3/6 to 6/6 pairs reach significance depending on window length---confirming that its elevated FNR reflects limited residual degrees of freedom rather than absence of signal.
41	
42	\paragraph{Effective rank direction is model-dependent.}
43	Effective rank evolves in opposite directions across architectures during iterative training.
44	In Pythia-410M, effective rank decreases (representational contraction), consistent with mode collapse concentrating the output distribution into fewer dimensions.
45	In GPT-2-medium at $\alpha{=}0.25$, effective rank \emph{increases} from 2.37 to 8.48 (+258\%; \texttt{gen\_wiki\_gpt2m\_a025\_s42}).
46	The likely explanation is starting-point dependence: GPT-2-medium begins near rank-2 (a highly concentrated initial representation), and iterative training disperses activation mass across more dimensions rather than concentrating it further.
47	This finding demonstrates that effective rank cannot serve as a direction-agnostic canary and that its trajectory must be interpreted relative to architecture-specific baselines.
48	We note this as a caveat on the generality of representational metrics as collapse indicators, even when the regime map (Table~\ref{tab:cross_arch_regime}) and lead-lag detectability are consistent across architectures.
49	
50	\paragraph{Diversity--calibration dissociation.}
51	Diversity metrics and calibration metrics respond to synthetic contamination in qualitatively opposite directions across the $\alpha$ spectrum.
52	Diversity collapse (distinct-$n$ decline) is most severe at low contamination ($\alpha{=}0.25$: distinct-3 declines by $58.9{\pm}0.9\%$) and attenuates at higher levels ($\alpha{=}1.0$: $-30.5\%$), while calibration degradation (ECE) increases monotonically with $\alpha$ ($+51.7\%$ at $\alpha{=}0.0$ to $+3780\%$ at $\alpha{=}1.0$).
53	This dissociation underscores the need for multi-method consensus: the regime in which a metric degrades most severely ($\alpha{=}0.25$ for diversity) does not coincide with the regime in which its temporal lead is most detectable ($\alpha{\geq}0.75$), and a single metric family cannot capture the full picture of iterative training degradation.
54	
55	\paragraph{Practical utility paradox.}
56	The canary framework exhibits a paradox: it works best where it is least needed.
57	At $\alpha{\geq}0.75$, token entropy reliably Granger-precedes diversity collapse---but at these contamination levels, degradation is already severe and likely detectable through simpler means.
58	At $\alpha{\leq}0.50$, where early warning would be most valuable, the canary signal is undetectable by any method tested.
59	This is not a framework deficiency but an inherent characteristic of collapse dynamics: at low contamination, diversity changes are too small relative to inter-generation noise for any temporal lead to emerge above the statistical detection floor~\cite{boettiger2012limits}.
60	The practical implication is that the framework is better suited to continuous monitoring dashboards---tracking cumulative trend shifts across many generations---than to single-point threshold alarms.
61	If contamination grows over time (as in realistic web-scale data pipelines), the canary signal will strengthen as $\alpha$ increases, providing earlier detection than post-hoc quality audits.
62	
63	\paragraph{Token entropy versus perplexity.}
64	The finding that token entropy Granger-precedes diversity while perplexity does not ($F{\approx}0$, $p{=}0.99$ after differencing) requires clarification, since perplexity is the exponential of cross-entropy and appears mathematically related to token entropy.
```

```text
180	Its classification as ``secondary'' is driven entirely by Toda--Yamamoto Type~II errors at $T{=}10$, where bootstrap power analysis yields estimated power of 0.05--0.20 depending on $d_{\max}$.
181	Resolving ECE's true canary status requires longer time series.
182	
183	\paragraph{Failed canary candidates.}
184	Not all metrics serve as canaries.
185	Effective rank and Self-BLEU show no detectable lead-lag relationship with any downstream metric under any method or contamination level, and are excluded from the consensus framework.
186	Effective rank behavior is model-dependent in an unexpected way: in Pythia-410M, effective rank decreases during collapse (representational contraction), whereas in GPT-2-medium at $\alpha{=}0.25$ it \emph{increases} from 2.37 to 8.48 (+258\%; \texttt{gen\_wiki\_gpt2m\_a025\_s42}).
187	The GPT-2-medium starting effective rank is near rank-2, suggesting the model's initial representation is already highly concentrated; iterative training disperses it rather than concentrating it further.
188	This opposite directionality across architectures precludes effective rank as a universal canary and motivates architecture-specific analysis (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
189	
190	\paragraph{Consensus filtering.}
191	Of 16 canary$\to$downstream pairs tested at $\alpha{=}1.0$ (4 candidate canaries $\times$ 4 downstream metrics), \textbf{6 survive} the full consensus pipeline ($K{\geq}4/5$ methods, $S{\geq}3/4$ seeds): token entropy and ECE each predicting distinct-1 and distinct-2 (4 pairs; distinct-3 results in Appendix~\ref{app:distinct3} bring the total to~6).
192	The 10 eliminated pairs comprise all canary$\to$perplexity relationships (identified as spurious by the first-differencing diagnostic) and all effective-rank and Self-BLEU pairs (failed canaries).
193	The consensus framework reduces the naive 100\% significance rate to the 37.5\% rate supported by the most conservative individual methods (Figure~\ref{fig:consensus_workflow}).
194
```

```json
    "originalFile": "% 2026-05-16: Added GPT-2m α=0.25 cross-architecture regime map table (tab:cross_arch_regime),\n% updated \"Failed canary candidates\" with effective rank model-dependence (+258% in GPT-2m).\n% Source: gen_wiki_gpt2m_a025_s42 registry entry.\n\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\nWe study lead-lag analysis pitfalls in iterative training collapse using Pythia-410M \\cite{biderman2023pythia}, a 410M-parameter decoder-only language model.\nPythia provides a controlled setting: small enough for multi-seed experiments across five contamination levels, yet large enough to exhibit meaningful generation-over-generation degradation.\nCross-architecture validation uses GPT-2-medium (345M parameters, also decoder-only), a second transformer family of similar scale; results are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:method_sensitivity}, \\S\\ref{sec:regime}, and \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}.\nTraining data is drawn from WikiText-103 \\cite{merity2017pointer}, using 5{,}000-sample subsets per generation.\n\nOur protocol follows the replace paradigm of \\citet{shumailov2024curse}, adapted for mixed synthetic-real training at $\\alpha{<}1.0$.\nEach generation $t$ proceeds in three steps: (1)~fine-tune the generation-$(t{-}1)$ model for 5{,}000 steps on a mixture of $\\alpha$ fraction synthetic data and $(1{-}\\alpha)$ fraction real data (AdamW optimizer, learning rate $5 \\times 10^{-5}$, 500 warmup steps, weight decay $0.01$, batch size 4 for Pythia-410M, 32 for GPT-2-medium); (2)~generate 5{,}000 synthetic samples from the fine-tuned model (temperature $1.0$, top-$k{=}50$, max length 512 tokens); (3)~fully replace the synthetic pool.\nWe run $T{=}10$ generations (11 time points including the generation-0 baseline) across contamination levels $\\alpha \\in \\{0.00, 0.25, 0.50, 0.75, 1.00\\}$.\nThe $\\alpha{=}1.0$ condition uses four random seeds $\\{42, 43, 44, 45\\}$; $\\alpha{=}0.25$ uses three seeds $\\{42, 43, 44\\}$; other conditions use seed~42.\n\nWe track two \\emph{canary metrics}---cheap-to-compute statistics hypothesized to precede downstream degradation, inspired by early-warning signals in dynamical systems \\cite{scheffer2009early}---and three \\emph{downstream metrics}.\nCanary metrics are token entropy (next-token prediction entropy averaged over held-out samples) and expected calibration error (ECE).\nDownstream metrics are distinct-1 and distinct-2 ($n$-gram diversity \\cite{li2016diversity}, computed over all 5{,}000 generated samples per generation) and perplexity (evaluated on held-out real data).\nToken entropy is the mean next-token prediction entropy $H(p_\\theta | \\text{context})$ averaged over 1{,}000 held-out samples; ECE uses 15 equal-width bins.\nWe additionally evaluate effective rank and Self-BLEU as candidate canaries (\\S\\ref{sec:case_study}).\nResults for distinct-3 are reported in Appendix~\\ref{app:distinct3}.\nCode and data will be released upon publication.\n\nAll hypothesis tests apply Benjamini--Hochberg (BH) FDR correction at $q{=}0.05$.\nPermutation Granger causality tests use 5{,}000 permutations on first-differenced series \\cite{shojaie2022granger}.\nCross-correlation analysis uses a maximum lag of 5 generations.\nTable~\\ref{tab:drift_summary} summarizes raw metric drift from generation~0 to generation~10 across all five contamination levels.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Metric drift (\\%, generation~0$\\to$10) for Pythia-410M across contamination levels. Diversity collapse occurs at all $\\alpha$ levels, with the strongest decline at moderate contamination ($\\alpha{=}0.25$: distinct-1 $-40.3{\\pm}0.3\\%$) rather than full replacement ($\\alpha{=}1.0$: $-27.8{\\pm}1.6\\%$). $\\alpha{=}0.25$: 3-seed mean${\\pm}$std (seeds 42, 43, 44); $\\alpha{=}1.0$: 4-seed mean${\\pm}$std; other levels: single seed (seed=42).}\n\\label{tab:drift_summary}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n$\\alpha$ & Entropy & ECE & Distinct-1 & Distinct-2 & Perplexity \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.00 & $-5.1$ & $+51.7$ & $-13.8$ & $-9.8$ & $-21.7$ \\\\\n0.25 & $-4.7{\\pm}0.5$ & $+81.8{\\pm}15.9$ & $-40.3{\\pm}0.3$ & $-56.4{\\pm}0.7$ & $-18.1{\\pm}0.8$ \\\\\n0.50 & $-18.6$ & $+842.8$ & $-37.3$ & $-51.5$ & $+21.1$ \\\\\n0.75 & $-27.7$ & $+1809.5$ & $-32.7$ & $-41.3$ & $+182.1$ \\\\\n1.00 & $-60.8{\\pm}1.2$ & $+3780{\\pm}30$ & $-27.8{\\pm}1.6$ & $-34.0{\\pm}3.3$ & $+3674{\\pm}45$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Method Sensitivity Analysis}\n\\label{sec:method_sensitivity}\n\nWe open with Claim~3---method sensitivity---because it conditions the interpretation of all subsequent results.\nIf different statistical methods yield contradictory conclusions on the same data, any single-method analysis is unreliable by construction.\n\n\\paragraph{Five-method ablation.}\nWe apply five lead-lag detection methods to the $\\alpha{=}1.0$ condition (4~seeds, 16 canary$\\to$downstream pairs).\nThe fraction of pairs reaching BH-corrected FDR significance varies dramatically by method: naive cross-correlation \\textbf{16/16} (100\\%), differenced cross-correlation \\textbf{14/16} (87.5\\%), threshold onset detection \\textbf{9/16} (56.3\\%), permutation Granger causality \\textbf{6/16} (37.5\\%), and Toda--Yamamoto Granger \\textbf{4/16} (25.0\\%).\nMethod choice alone swings significance from \\textbf{100\\% to 25\\%}: 50--57\\% of pairs classified as significant under one method flip to non-significant under another.\nThe spurious-signal rate---the fraction of pairs detected by the most liberal method but not confirmed by the most conservative---is \\textbf{75\\%} across all five methods and \\textbf{62.5\\%} when excluding Toda--Yamamoto, whose low statistical power at $T{=}10$ inflates this rate (\\S\\ref{sec:consensus_validation}).\nTable~\\ref{tab:method_comparison} reports significance rates restricted to 8 validated canary$\\to$downstream pairs (excluding failed candidates); the 16-pair rates above include effective rank and Self-BLEU to capture the full method-sensitivity landscape.\n\n\\paragraph{First-differencing diagnostic.}\nA single preprocessing decision can eliminate an entire analysis channel.\nFor canary$\\to$diversity pairs at $\\alpha{=}1.0$, all pairs retain unanimous FDR significance after first-differencing (24/24$\\to$24/24), confirming genuine temporal precedence rather than shared-trend confounding.\nFor canary$\\to$perplexity, however, undifferenced tests find near-universal significance ($F{=}16$--$36$) while differenced tests find \\textbf{zero} significant pairs ($F{\\approx}0$, $p{=}0.99$; Figure~\\ref{fig:differencing_dmax}a).\nThe apparent canary$\\to$perplexity relationship is entirely an artifact of co-trending.\n\n\\paragraph{Augmentation order sensitivity.}\nToda--Yamamoto Granger tests show extreme sensitivity to the augmentation order $d_{\\max}$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:differencing_dmax}b).\nAt $d_{\\max}{=}0$ (most permissive), approximately 6\\% of pairs are rejected; at adaptive $d_{\\max}$ determined by ADF unit-root tests, 75\\%; at $d_{\\max}{=}1$ (most conservative), \\textbf{94\\%}.\nThis single hyperparameter swings conclusions from near-universal significance to near-universal non-significance.\n\n\\paragraph{Inter-method agreement.}\nCohen's $\\kappa$, averaged over all method pairs, is \\textbf{0.185} (near chance; poor agreement); this value is consistent across contamination levels ($\\alpha{=}0.0$ through $\\alpha{=}1.0$), indicating that method disagreement is a structural property of the analysis rather than a regime-specific artifact.\nCross-architecture validation on GPT-2-medium (12 canary$\\to$downstream pairs) yields Fleiss' $\\kappa{=}0.091$, also in the ``slight agreement'' range, confirming that low inter-method agreement is not architecture-specific.\nRestricting to three informative methods (excluding naive cross-correlation and Toda--Yamamoto, which anchor the permissive and conservative extremes respectively) raises $\\kappa$ to 0.667 (substantial agreement), indicating that the low full-panel $\\kappa$ is driven by the deliberate inclusion of methods spanning the entire permissive-to-conservative spectrum rather than by fundamental incoherence among methods.\nTable~\\ref{tab:method_comparison} extends the analysis across contamination levels.\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.0$ (no synthetic data, serving as null control), naive cross-correlation finds \\textbf{100\\%} of pairs significant---a textbook spurious-regression outcome in trending time series.\nPermutation Granger and Toda--Yamamoto correctly find 0\\%.\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.50$, correlation-based methods (naive, differenced, threshold) unanimously find significance while causal methods unanimously find non-significance (Figure~\\ref{fig:consensus_heatmap}), the most extreme method$\\times$regime divergence in our experiments.\n\n\\paragraph{Method$\\times$regime interaction.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:method_comparison} reveals that the correlation-versus-causal divergence is not uniform across contamination levels.\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.25$, differenced cross-correlation drops to 25\\% and threshold onset to 37.5\\%, while naive cross-correlation remains at 100\\%---demonstrating that even partial detrending removes marginal associations at low contamination.\nIn low-contamination regimes ($\\alpha{\\leq}0.50$), the false confirmation rate for the most liberal method approaches 100\\%, while all Granger-type tests correctly find 0\\%---a regime where single-method analysis is maximally dangerous.\nThe graded response across methods, combined with the block structure visible in Figure~\\ref{fig:consensus_heatmap}, motivates the regime-dependent analysis in \\S\\ref{sec:regime}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{FDR significance rates (\\%) for five lead-lag methods across contamination levels (Pythia-410M). Method choice swings significance from 100\\% (naive cross-correlation) to 0\\% (permutation Granger) at $\\alpha{\\leq}0.50$, demonstrating that single-method conclusions are unreliable. Each cell reports the fraction of 8 canary$\\to$downstream pairs (token entropy and ECE $\\times$ 4 downstream metrics) reaching BH-corrected significance ($q{=}0.05$), excluding failed candidates (\\S\\ref{sec:case_study}). Seed coverage: $\\alpha{=}1.0$: 4 seeds (32 pairs); $\\alpha{=}0.25$ Toda--Yamamoto: 3 seeds (24 pairs), other methods single seed; remaining columns: single seed. $\\alpha{=}0.75$ analyzed separately (\\S\\ref{sec:regime}).}\n\\label{tab:method_comparison}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & $\\alpha{=}0.0$ & $\\alpha{=}0.25$ & $\\alpha{=}0.50$ & $\\alpha{=}0.75$ & $\\alpha{=}1.0$ & FPR$_\\text{null}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nNaive XCorr     & 100   & 100  & 100  & ---  & 100   & 100  \\\\\nDiff.\\ XCorr   & 37.5  & 25   & 100  & ---  & 100   & 37.5 \\\\\nThreshold       & 62.5  & 37.5 & 100  & ---  & 100   & 62.5 \\\\\nPerm.\\ Granger  & 0     & 0    & 0    & ---  & 75    & 0    \\\\\nToda--Yamamoto  & 0     & 12.5 & 0    & ---  & 59.4  & 0    \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Regime-Dependent Detectability}\n\\label{sec:regime}\n\nBeyond method choice, the contamination level~$\\alpha$ determines whether a temporal lead exists to detect.\nWe present regime effects at two levels: raw metric drift (method-independent) and Granger-precedence detectability.\n\n\\paragraph{Raw drift.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:drift_summary} shows that diversity collapse occurs at \\emph{all} contamination levels, not only under full synthetic replacement.\nDistinct-1 declines by 13.8\\% even at $\\alpha{=}0.0$ (attributable to fine-tuning on repeated real-data subsets), and more steeply at intermediate contamination: $-40.3{\\pm}0.3\\%$ ($\\alpha{=}0.25$), $-37.3\\%$ ($\\alpha{=}0.50$), $-32.7\\%$ ($\\alpha{=}0.75$), and $-27.8{\\pm}1.6\\%$ ($\\alpha{=}1.0$).\nThe non-monotonic pattern---diversity declining \\emph{more} at moderate~$\\alpha$ than at extreme~$\\alpha$---is confirmed by 3-seed replication at $\\alpha{=}0.25$ (std${=}0.3\\%$), indicating that this relationship between contamination level and collapse severity is robust rather than a seed-specific artifact.\n\n\\paragraph{Granger-precedence detectability.}\nDespite ubiquitous drift, causal detectability is sharply regime-dependent (Figure~\\ref{fig:regime_map}).\nPermutation Granger tests (first-differenced, BH-corrected) find 0/8 pairs FDR-significant at $\\alpha{=}0.00$, 0/8 at $\\alpha{=}0.25$, 0/8 at $\\alpha{=}0.50$, and \\textbf{6/8} at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ in the cross-$\\alpha$ pooled analysis.\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.75$ (single seed, seed=42), 3/8 pairs reach FDR significance; this result requires multi-seed replication before serving as primary evidence.\nWithin-$\\alpha$ multi-seed analysis at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ yields \\textbf{24/24} canary$\\to$diversity pairs FDR-significant across all four seeds, with 0/24 in the reverse direction, confirming temporal asymmetry.\nThe detectability boundary---the regime below which canary metrics no longer develop sufficient temporal lead for causal inference---lies at $\\alpha \\approx 0.50$--$0.75$, consistent with theoretical expectations about signal-to-noise limits in short time series \\cite{boettiger2012limits}.\nThis boundary is sharp rather than gradual: method-level consensus ($K{\\geq}4/5$) identifies 0--1 of 16 candidate pairs as robust at $\\alpha{\\leq}0.50$ but 11/16 at $\\alpha{=}0.75$ (single seed, pre-pipeline filtering; the stricter $K{\\geq}4/5$, $S{\\geq}3/4$ criterion at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ retains 6/16 after excluding failed candidates and spurious channels per \\S\\ref{sec:case_study}).\nThe lower count at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ despite stronger contamination reflects the stricter validation standard: multi-seed replication ($S{\\geq}3/4$) filters seed-specific artifacts, and pipeline filtering removes the perplexity channel (spurious per \\S\\ref{sec:method_sensitivity}) and failed canary candidates (effective rank, Self-BLEU), both of which contribute to the $\\alpha{=}0.75$ single-seed count.\nCross-architecture validation on GPT-2-medium confirms this boundary: at $\\alpha{=}0.25$, 0/8 pairs reach Confirmatory consensus ($K{\\geq}4/5$) despite substantially stronger canary drift (entropy $-32.8\\%$ vs.\\ Pythia $-4.7{\\pm}0.5\\%$), while at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ all 6 canary$\\to$diversity pairs reach $K{\\geq}4/5$ (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_arch_regime} summarizes the regime map across both architectures.\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.25$, GPT-2-medium yields 1/8 pairs at Tier~3 ($K{\\geq}4/5$) and 7/8 at Tier~2 ($K{\\geq}3/5$)---consistent with Pythia's 0/8 Tier~3, 0/8 Tier~2 result at the same contamination level, placing both architectures in the null/low-detectability regime (\\texttt{gen\\_wiki\\_gpt2m\\_a025\\_s42}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-architecture regime map: lead-lag consensus tier and metric drift at $\\alpha{=}0.25$ and $\\alpha{=}1.0$. Tier~3: $K{\\geq}4/5$ methods; Tier~2: $K{\\geq}3/5$. Drift is generation~0$\\to$10. GPT-2-medium $\\alpha{=}0.25$: single seed (seed=42, \\texttt{gen\\_wiki\\_gpt2m\\_a025\\_s42}); Pythia $\\alpha{=}0.25$: 3-seed mean${\\pm}$std. The regime boundary ($\\alpha{\\approx}0.50$--$0.75$) is consistent across two transformer decoder-only model families of similar scale.}\n\\label{tab:cross_arch_regime}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccccc}\n\\toprule\n$\\alpha$ & Model & Entropy & ECE & Distinct-1 & Distinct-2 & Perm.\\ Granger & Tier~3 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{0.25} & Pythia-410M & $-4.7{\\pm}0.5$ & $+81.8{\\pm}15.9$ & $-40.3{\\pm}0.3$ & $-56.4{\\pm}0.7$ & 0/8 & 0/8 \\\\\n                      & GPT-2-medium & $-32.8$ & $+422$ & $-23.5$ & $-31.6$ & 0/8 & 1/8 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{1.00} & Pythia-410M & $-60.8{\\pm}1.2$ & $+3780{\\pm}30$ & $-27.8{\\pm}1.6$ & $-34.0{\\pm}3.3$ & 6/8 & 6/6 \\\\\n                      & GPT-2-medium & \\multicolumn{5}{c}{(reported in \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}; all 6 canary$\\to$diversity Tier~3)} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Synchronous collapse at $\\alpha{=}0.50$.}\nThe $\\alpha{=}0.50$ regime provides particularly informative evidence.\nCorrelation-based methods unanimously find significance (Table~\\ref{tab:method_comparison}) while causal methods unanimously find non-significance.\nThis extreme method$\\times$regime interaction arises because canary and downstream metrics collapse \\emph{synchronously} at moderate contamination, preserving high correlation but eliminating the temporal lead required for Granger-precedence inference.\nWe highlight this as a \\emph{positive finding}: it delineates the regime boundary where naive analysis is most dangerous---high correlation coexists with zero causal precedence, maximizing the risk of spurious conclusions.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:consensus_heatmap} visualizes this interaction across all method$\\times\\alpha$ combinations, revealing a clear block structure that separates correlation-based methods from causal methods as a function of regime.\n\n\n\\subsection{Case Study: Canary Prediction of Diversity Collapse}\n\\label{sec:case_study}\n\nHaving established method sensitivity (Claim~3) and regime dependence (Claim~2), we present the conditions under which canary prediction succeeds (Claim~1).\nWe scope this claim precisely: canary metrics predict \\emph{diversity collapse} (declining distinct-$n$), not model collapse broadly.\n\n\\paragraph{Full synthetic replacement ($\\alpha{=}1.0$, 4 seeds).}\nToken entropy exhibits robust Granger-precedence over diversity collapse metrics, confirmed by 4 independent statistical methods (permutation Granger power${=}1.0$), 4 seeds, and cross-architecture replication on GPT-2-medium (Tier~3 consensus; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\nCross-correlation peaks at lag$={+}1$ with $|r|{>}0.99$ (cross-seed standard deviation: 0.0), establishing a consistent one-generation lead.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:metric_trajectory} illustrates the one-generation offset between token entropy decline and distinct-1 decline, visible across all four seeds.\nPermutation Granger tests (first-differenced) confirm all canary$\\to$diversity pairs as FDR-significant across all four seeds, including distinct-3 (Appendix~\\ref{app:distinct3}).\nReverse-direction tests (downstream$\\to$canary) find \\textbf{0/24} FDR-significant (all $p{>}0.49$), establishing complete directional asymmetry.\n\n\\paragraph{High contamination ($\\alpha{=}0.75$, single seed).}\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.75$ (seed=42), three of four forward-direction pairs reach FDR significance: ECE$\\to$distinct-1 ($p_\\text{fdr}{=}0.046$), ECE$\\to$distinct-2 ($p_\\text{fdr}{=}0.046$), and token entropy$\\to$distinct-2 ($p_\\text{fdr}{=}0.048$).\nToken entropy$\\to$distinct-1 is borderline ($p_\\text{fdr}{=}0.054$).\nAll four reverse directions are non-significant ($p{>}0.49$).\nThese single-seed results require multi-seed confirmation before serving as primary evidence.\n\n\\paragraph{ECE as a conditional secondary canary.}\nECE performs equivalently to token entropy across four of five methods (naive, differenced, threshold, permutation Granger).\nIts classification as ``secondary'' is driven entirely by Toda--Yamamoto Type~II errors at $T{=}10$, where bootstrap power analysis yields estimated power of 0.05--0.20 depending on $d_{\\max}$.\nResolving ECE's true canary status requires longer time series.\n\n\\paragraph{Failed canary candidates.}\nNot all metrics serve as canaries.\nEffective rank and Self-BLEU show no detectable lead-lag relationship with any downstream metric under any method or contamination level, and are excluded from the consensus framework.\nEffective rank behavior is model-dependent in an unexpected way: in Pythia-410M, effective rank decreases during collapse (representational contraction), whereas in GPT-2-medium at $\\alpha{=}0.25$ it \\emph{increases} from 2.37 to 8.48 (+258\\%; \\texttt{gen\\_wiki\\_gpt2m\\_a025\\_s42}).\nThe GPT-2-medium starting effective rank is near rank-2, suggesting the model's initial representation is already highly concentrated; iterative training disperses it rather than concentrating it further.\nThis opposite directionality across architectures precludes effective rank as a universal canary and motivates architecture-specific analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n\\paragraph{Consensus filtering.}\nOf 16 canary$\\to$downstream pairs tested at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (4 candidate canaries $\\times$ 4 downstream metrics), \\textbf{6 survive} the full consensus pipeline ($K{\\geq}4/5$ methods, $S{\\geq}3/4$ seeds): token entropy and ECE each predicting distinct-1 and distinct-2 (4 pairs; distinct-3 results in Appendix~\\ref{app:distinct3} bring the total to~6).\nThe 10 eliminated pairs comprise all canary$\\to$perplexity relationships (identified as spurious by the first-differencing diagnostic) and all effective-rank and Self-BLEU pairs (failed canaries).\nThe consensus framework reduces the naive 100\\% significance rate to the 37.5\\% rate supported by the most conservative individual methods (Figure~\\ref{fig:consensus_workflow}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Consensus Framework Validation}\n\\label{sec:consensus_validation}\n\nWe validate the consensus framework's calibration using built-in experimental controls.\n\n\\paragraph{False positive rate.}\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.0$, no synthetic data is injected; any detected lead-lag association is a false positive.\nIndividual-method FPR ranges from \\textbf{100\\%} (naive cross-correlation: 8/8 pairs) through 62.5\\% (threshold) and 37.5\\% (differenced) down to \\textbf{0\\%} (permutation Granger and Toda--Yamamoto).\nThe consensus framework at $K{\\geq}4/5$ achieves \\textbf{FPR${=}$0\\%}, matching the best individual methods while providing robustness against miscalibration of any single method (Table~\\ref{tab:fpr_fnr}).\n\n\\paragraph{False negative rate.}\nAt $\\alpha{=}1.0$, we define 6 effect-size reference pairs (token entropy and ECE $\\to$ distinct-1/2/3) using criteria independent of the consensus methods: drift ratio ${\\geq}3\\times$ the $\\alpha{=}0.0$ baseline (entropy $11.93\\times$, ECE $73.12\\times$) and canary onset strictly preceding downstream onset by ${\\geq}1$ generation (\\S\\ref{sec:calibration}).\nFNR is calibrated on a 4-method panel (excluding differenced cross-correlation to avoid circularity): at $K{\\geq}3/4$, $S{\\geq}3/4$, all~6 are recovered (\\textbf{FNR${=}$0\\%}).\nToda--Yamamoto alone yields FNR${=}$33.3\\% (2/6), missing ECE$\\to$distinct-2 and ECE$\\to$distinct-3 due to Type~II errors at $T{=}10$.\nWe note a design asymmetry: FPR is validated on method-only data (single seed at $\\alpha{=}0.0$), while FNR uses method$\\times$seed joint data (4~seeds at $\\alpha{=}1.0$).\nFull joint calibration---multi-seed null-control experiments---remains future work.\nTable~\\ref{tab:fpr_fnr} reports the calibration results.\n\n\\paragraph{Bootstrap power analysis.}\nTo quantify detection power beyond binary FNR, we perform bootstrap power analysis ($B{=}1{,}000$ resamples from 4-seed data at $\\alpha{=}1.0$).\nPermutation Granger achieves power${=}1.0$ on genuine pairs (canary$\\to$diversity) and power${=}0.0$ on spurious pairs (canary$\\to$perplexity), providing clean separation.\nToda--Yamamoto achieves power${=}0.05$--$0.20$ on genuine pairs (depending on $d_{\\max}$), confirming its poor sensitivity at $T{=}10$.\nWe note that bootstrap power${=}1.0$ reflects the effect size in our 4-seed data and should not be interpreted as a universal power guarantee; furthermore, the two methods use different data-generating processes for calibration (permutation Granger resamples observed $\\alpha{=}1.0$ trajectories, while Toda--Yamamoto power was estimated under a generic VAR(2) simulation with $\\beta{=}0.3$).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{False positive and false negative rates for individual methods and the consensus framework (Pythia-410M). The consensus threshold $K{\\geq}4/5$ achieves FPR${=}$0\\% and FNR${=}$0\\%, matching the best individual method (permutation Granger) while providing robustness against miscalibration. FPR: $\\alpha{=}0.0$ null control (single seed, $M{=}5$ methods). FNR: $\\alpha{=}1.0$ against 6 effect-size reference pairs (4-method panel excluding differenced cross-correlation). Note dimension asymmetry: FPR is method-only; FNR is method${\\times}$seed joint (\\S\\ref{sec:consensus_validation}).}\n\\label{tab:fpr_fnr}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nCriterion & FPR ($\\alpha{=}0.0$) & FNR ($\\alpha{=}1.0$) \\\\\n\\midrule\nNaive XCorr              & 100\\%  & 0\\%  \\\\\nDifferenced XCorr        & 37.5\\% & 0\\%  \\\\\nThreshold Onset          & 62.5\\% & 0\\%  \\\\\nPermutation Granger      & 0\\%    & 0\\%  \\\\\nToda--Yamamoto           & 0\\%    & 33.3\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\nConsensus ($K{\\geq}4/5$)              & 0\\%    & 0\\%  \\\\\nConsensus ($K{\\geq}4/5$, $S{\\geq}3/4$) & 0\\%    & 0\\%  \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}",
```
